    "originalFile": "\n\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} summarizes TSR and CAR across conditions (50 rollouts $\\times$ 10 tasks per seed). $\\Delta$CAR is computed against the matched-codebase Vanilla baseline (v2: $0.745 \\pm 0.024$, 3 seeds, for factorial conditions above the midrule; v1: 0.780 for supplementary conditions below). Random OCC ($^\\star$) uses v1 code but is included above the midrule as a factorial control; its $\\Delta$CAR is omitted due to the cross-codebase baseline mismatch. Statistical comparisons use permutation tests and Cohen's $d$ against the Vanilla baseline.\n\n\\textbf{Gradient flow interacts with normalization type.} The four factorial cells reveal a normalization-dependent pattern. Under BatchNorm, gradient flow degrades safety: Full OCC (BN, gradient ON) is the worst-performing cell (CAR $0.725$, $\\Delta{=}-2.0$\\,pp vs.\\ Vanilla) and the sole configuration falling below baseline, while Detach OCC (gradient OFF) exceeds it ($0.767$, $\\Delta{=}+2.2$\\,pp). Under LayerNorm, gradient flow is benign: Full OCC+LN ($0.773$, $\\Delta{=}+2.8$\\,pp) already exceeds Vanilla, and Detach+LN ($0.802 \\pm 0.024$, $n{=}3$) yields the highest cell mean ($\\Delta{=}+5.7$\\,pp). Both LN cells exceed the Vanilla baseline, whereas only the gradient-OFF BN cell does so. The BN + gradient flow cell also exhibits the highest instability ($\\sigma{=}0.064$, ${\\geq}\\,2{\\times}$ all other cells). Spatial label content does not detectably affect outcomes: Full OCC (ground-truth occupancy), Random OCC (random binary labels), and Depth (egocentric depth maps) all fall within cross-seed variance ($p > 0.4$, observed under v1 codebase for Random OCC and Depth).\n\n\\textbf{BN + gradient flow destabilizes training.} Full OCC (BN) exhibits the highest variance of any factorial cell ($\\sigma{=}0.064$, ${\\geq}\\,2{\\times}$ that of all other cells; $\\sigma \\leq 0.027$ for all other cells): individual seeds range from $0.776$ (near Vanilla) to $0.654$ ($\\Delta{=}-9.1$\\,pp), spanning $12.2$\\,pp. This instability is independent of the mean deficit and indicates a volatile optimization landscape specific to the BN + gradient flow combination. No other cell approaches this variance, suggesting that BatchNorm's running-statistics mechanism amplifies stochastic sensitivity when auxiliary gradients flow through the shared encoders.\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Results on SafeLIBERO Level~I (10 tasks, 50 rollouts/seed). Mean $\\pm$ SD across seeds. $\\Delta$CAR vs.\\ v2 Vanilla ($0.745$) for factorial conditions; vs.\\ v1 Vanilla ($0.780$) for supplementary. $^{a}$Highest variance. $^\\ddagger$2 seeds. $^\\star$v1 \\\\\nON  & BN (Cutoff@45K)       & 1 & $0.764$           & $0.896$           & $-$1.6 \\\\\nON  & BN (Rev.\\ Cutoff)$^\\ddagger$ & 2 & $0.718 \\pm 0.025$ & $0.839 \\pm 0.072$ & $-$6.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Normalization and Gradient Flow Effects}\n\\label{sec:mechanism}\n\nThe four factorial cells form a $2{\\times}2$ design crossing normalization type (BN/LN) with gradient flow (ON/OFF) (Table~\\ref{tab:factorial}, Figure~\\ref{fig:factorial}).\n\n\\textbf{Gradient flow is harmful under BatchNorm but benign under LayerNorm.} The BN row provides the clearest signal: gradient flow reduces CAR by 4.2\\,pp (Full OCC $0.725 \\pm 0.064$ vs.\\ Detach OCC $0.767 \\pm 0.024$; Cohen's $d{=}-0.93$, large). Full OCC (BN) is the sole factorial cell falling below Vanilla ($\\Delta{=}-2.0$\\,pp), whereas Detach OCC recovers above it ($\\Delta{=}+2.2$\\,pp, $d{=}+0.88$, large). In the LN row, Full OCC+LN already exceeds Vanilla ($0.773$, $\\Delta{=}+2.8$\\,pp, $d{=}+1.07$, large), and Detach+LN yields the highest cell mean ($0.802 \\pm 0.024$, $n{=}3$, $\\Delta{=}+5.7$\\,pp, $d{=}+2.36$, very large). Two main effects drive the factorial pattern ($2{\\times}2$ ANOVA: gradient flow $F(1,10){=}3.20$, $p{=}0.104$, $\\eta^2{=}0.18$; normalization $F(1,10){=}4.49$, $p{=}0.060$, $\\eta^2{=}0.25$; interaction $F{<}0.1$, $p{=}0.76$; Appendix~\\ref{app:stat_power}). First, gradient flow under BN reduces CAR by 4.2\\,pp ($d{=}0.93$, large), making BN + gradient flow the sole configuration below the Vanilla baseline. Second, LN provides a consistent safety advantage over BN: $+$4.8\\,pp under gradient flow ($d{=}1.05$, large) and $+$3.5\\,pp without ($d{=}1.47$, very large). This is a practical threshold crossing (only the BN + gradient flow combination harms safety) rather than a statistical interaction (the factorial interaction is negligible, $\\eta^2{=}0.005$).\n\nPairwise Welch $t$-tests with Bonferroni correction yield no significant comparisons at $\\alpha{=}0.05$, consistent with small per-cell $n$ ($n{=}3$--$4$); we rely on effect sizes rather than $p$-values for inference (Appendix~\\ref{app:stat_power}). The normalization main effect approaches significance ($p{=}0.060$). Both main effects amplify by ${\\approx}3{\\times}$ on the three physically constrained tasks (T3, T5, T6; Appendix~\\ref{app:per_task_factorial}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{$2{\\times}2$ factorial: normalization type $\\times$ gradient flow (all v2 data). Two main effects: gradient flow under BN $d{=}0.93$ (large); normalization effect $d{=}1.03$ (large). BN + gradient flow is the sole cell below Vanilla. $^{b}$Cell~4 preliminary ($n{=}1$); additional seeds in progress.}\n\\label{tab:factorial}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n & Gradient ON & Gradient OFF & $\\Delta$(ON$-$OFF) \\\\\n\\midrule\nBatchNorm    & Full OCC (0.725) & Detach OCC (0.767) & $-$4.2 \\\\\nLayerNorm    & Full OCC+LN (0.777) & Detach+LN ($0.826^{b}$) & $-$4.9$^{b}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\Delta$(BN$-$LN) & $-$5.2 ($d{=}{-}1.03$) & $-$5.9$^{b}$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\textwidth]{figures/factorial_2x2.pdf}\n\\caption{$2{\\times}2$ factorial comparison: normalization type (BN/LN) $\\times$ gradient flow (ON/OFF). BN row: gradient flow reduces CAR by 4.2\\,pp ($d{=}0.93$); LN row: 4.9\\,pp ($n{=}1$, preliminary). All LN cells exceed Vanilla baseline. Cell~4 (Detach+LN) $n{=}1$; additional seeds in progress. Error bars: cross-seed SD where $n{\\geq}2$.}\n\\label{fig:factorial}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Gradient Dynamics}\n\nGradient analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:gradient_analysis}, Appendix) examines the mechanism through which auxiliary heads affect safety. All gradient norms are computed with respect to the L1 action loss (action tokens only).\n\n\\textbf{Gradient orthogonality rules out direct conflict.} Action--auxiliary gradient cosine similarity is near zero across all encoder layers for both Full OCC and Random OCC, with conflict rates of ${\\approx}$46\\%. Action loss gradient norms are comparable across Vanilla and gradient-flow conditions, ruling out gradient amplification. Both patterns are identical for structured and random labels, consistent with the behavioral finding that spatial content did not detectably affect outcomes in our experiments. (Random OCC gradient data were obtained under the v1 codebase; the v2 gradient analysis reported here covers all four factorial conditions and Vanilla, each using a v2-trained checkpoint.)\n\n\\textbf{Mechanism remains open.} The observed effect pattern cannot be explained by gradient conflict (orthogonality rules it out) or gradient amplification (norms are comparable). The detrimental effect likely operates through indirect modification of the LoRA optimization trajectory under a dual-objective loss landscape. CKA analysis~\\cite{kornblith2019similarity} confirms that encoder representations remain intact under all conditions (CKA $\\geq$ 0.95 for both DINOv2 and SigLIP), localizing the safety degradation to optimization dynamics rather than representational corruption.\n\nAdditional observations (per-task breakdown, depth comparison, and temporal dynamics) are in Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}.\n",

Mean $\\pm$ SD across seeds. $\\Delta$CAR vs.\\ v2 Vanilla ($0.745$) for factorial conditions; vs.\\ v1 Vanilla ($0.780$) for supplementary. $^{a}$Highest variance. $^\\ddagger$2 seeds. $^\\star$v1 code.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccl}\n\\toprule\nFlow & Norm & $n$ & CAR & TSR & $\\Delta$CAR \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{$2{\\times}2$ factorial (v2 code)}} \\\\\nON  & BN & 3 & $0.725 \\pm 0.064^{a}$ & $0.854 \\pm 0.052$ & $-$2.0 \\\\\nOFF & BN & 4 & $0.767 \\pm 0.024$ & $0.908 \\pm 0.026$ & $+$2.2 \\\\\nON  & LN & 4 & $0.773 \\pm 0.027$ & $0.914 \\pm 0.049$ & $+$2.8 \\\\\nOFF & LN & 3 & $0.802 \\pm 0.024$ & $0.911 \\pm 0.018$ & $+$5.7 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n---  & Vanilla & 3 & $0.745 \\pm 0.024$ & $0.878 \\pm 0.032$ & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Controls \\& supplementary (v1 code)}} \\\\\nON  & BN (Random)$^\\star$ & 3 & $0.789 \\pm 0.031$ & $0.869 \\pm 0.029$ & --- \\\\\nON  & --- (Depth)$^\\star$ & 4 & $0.780 \\pm 0.039$ & $0.889 \\pm 0.042$ & $0.0$ \\\\\nON  & BN (Cutoff@15K)$^\\ddagger$ & 2 & $0.782 \\pm 0.031$ & $0.883 \\pm 0.041$ & $+$0.2 \\\\\nON  & BN (Cutoff@25K)$^\\ddagger$ & 2 & $0.724 \\pm 0.051$ & $0.887 \\pm 0.061$ & $-$5.6 \\\\\nON  & BN (Cutoff@35K)       & 3 & $0.779 \\pm 0.017$ & $0.933 \\pm 0.032$ & $-$0.1 \\\\\nON  & BN (Cutoff@45K)       & 1 & $0.764$           & $0.896$           & $-$1.6 \\\\\nON  & BN (Rev.\\ Cutoff)$^\\ddagger$ & 2 & $0.718 \\pm 0.025$ & $0.839 \\pm 0.072$ & $-$6.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Normalization and Gradient Flow Effects}\n\\label{sec:mechanism}\n\nThe four factorial cells form a $2{\\times}2$ design crossing normalization type (BN/LN) with gradient flow (ON/OFF) (Table~\\ref{tab:factorial}, Figure~\\ref{fig:factorial}).\n\n\\textbf{Gradient flow is harmful under BatchNorm but benign under LayerNorm.} The BN row provides the clearest signal: gradient flow reduces CAR by 4.2\\,pp (Full OCC $0.725 \\pm 0.064$ vs.\\ Detach OCC $0.767 \\pm 0.024$; Cohen's $d{=}-0.93$, large). Full OCC (BN) is the sole factorial cell falling below Vanilla ($\\Delta{=}-2.0$\\,pp), whereas Detach OCC recovers above it ($\\Delta{=}+2.2$\\,pp, $d{=}+0.88$, large). In the LN row, Full OCC+LN already exceeds Vanilla ($0.773$, $\\Delta{=}+2.8$\\,pp, $d{=}+1.07$, large), and Detach+LN yields the highest cell mean ($0.802 \\pm 0.024$, $n{=}3$, $\\Delta{=}+5.7$\\,pp, $d{=}+2.36$, very large). Two main effects drive the factorial pattern ($2{\\times}2$ ANOVA: gradient flow $F(1,10){=}3.20$, $p{=}0.104$, $\\eta^2{=}0.18$; normalization $F(1,10){=}4.49$, $p{=}0.060$, $\\eta^2{=}0.25$; interaction $F{<}0.1$, $p{=}0.76$; Appendix~\\ref{app:stat_power}). First, gradient flow under BN reduces CAR by 4.2\\,pp ($d{=}0.93$, large), making BN + gradient flow the sole configuration below the Vanilla baseline. Second, LN provides a consistent safety advantage over BN: $+$4.8\\,pp under gradient flow ($d{=}1.05$, large) and $+$3.5\\,pp without ($d{=}1.47$, very large). This is a practical threshold crossing (only the BN + gradient flow combination harms safety) rather than a statistical interaction (the factorial interaction is negligible, $\\eta^2{=}0.005$).\n\nPairwise Welch $t$-tests with Bonferroni correction yield no significant comparisons at $\\alpha{=}0.05$, consistent with small per-cell $n$ ($n{=}3$--$4$); we rely on effect sizes rather than $p$-values for inference (Appendix~\\ref{app:stat_power}). The normalization main effect approaches significance ($p{=}0.060$). Both main effects amplify by ${\\approx}3{\\times}$ on the three physically constrained tasks (T3, T5, T6; Appendix~\\ref{app:per_task_factorial}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{$2{\\times}2$ factorial: normalization type $\\times$ gradient flow (all v2 data). Two main effects: gradient flow under BN $d{=}0.93$ (large); normalization effect $d{=}1.05$--$1.47$ (large to very large). BN + gradient flow is the sole cell below Vanilla. ANOVA interaction $F{<}0.1$, $p{=}0.76$.}\n\\label{tab:factorial}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n & Gradient ON & Gradient OFF & $\\Delta$(ON$-$OFF) \\\\\n\\midrule\nBatchNorm    & Full OCC (0.725) & Detach OCC (0.767) & $-$4.2 \\\\\nLayerNorm    & Full OCC+LN (0.773) & Detach+LN (0.802) & $-$2.9 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\Delta$(BN$-$LN) & $-$4.8 ($d{=}{-}1.05$) & $-$3.5 ($d{=}{-}1.47$) & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\textwidth]{figures/factorial_2x2.pdf}\n\\caption{$2{\\times}2$ factorial comparison: normalization type (BN/LN) $\\times$ gradient flow (ON/OFF). BN row: gradient flow reduces CAR by 4.2\\,pp ($d{=}0.93$); LN row: 4.9\\,pp ($n{=}1$, preliminary). All LN cells exceed Vanilla baseline. Cell~4 (Detach+LN) $n{=}1$; additional seeds in progress. Error bars: cross-seed SD where $n{\\geq}2$.}\n\\label{fig:factorial}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Gradient Dynamics}\n\nGradient analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:gradient_analysis}, Appendix) examines the mechanism through which auxiliary heads affect safety. All gradient norms are computed with respect to the L1 action loss (action tokens only).\n\n\\textbf{Gradient orthogonality rules out direct conflict.} Action--auxiliary gradient cosine similarity is near zero across all encoder layers for both Full OCC and Random OCC, with conflict rates of ${\\approx}$46\\%. Action loss gradient norms are comparable across Vanilla and gradient-flow conditions, ruling out gradient amplification. Both patterns are identical for structured and random labels, consistent with the behavioral finding that spatial content did not detectably affect outcomes in our experiments. (Random OCC gradient data were obtained under the v1 codebase; the v2 gradient analysis reported here covers all four factorial conditions and Vanilla, each using a v2-trained checkpoint.)\n\n\\textbf{Mechanism remains open.} The observed effect pattern cannot be explained by gradient conflict (orthogonality rules it out) or gradient amplification (norms are comparable). The detrimental effect likely operates through indirect modification of the LoRA optimization trajectory under a dual-objective loss landscape. CKA analysis~\\cite{kornblith2019similarity} confirms that encoder representations remain intact under all conditions (CKA $\\geq$ 0.95 for both DINOv2 and SigLIP), localizing the safety degradation to optimization dynamics rather than representational corruption.\n\nAdditional observations (per-task breakdown, depth comparison, and temporal dynamics) are in Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}.\n",

Under Batch Normalization (BN), gradient flow degrades safety: Full OCC BN achieves a 0.725 Collision Avoidance Rate versus 0.767 for Detach BN (Cohen's $d = -0.93$, large), falling below the no-auxiliary-head Vanilla baseline (0.745). The BN + gradient flow cell is both the worst-performing and most unstable configuration ($\\sigma{=}0.064$, ${\\geq}\\,2{\\times}$ all other cells). Spatial label content (ground-truth occupancy, random binary labels, egocentric depth maps) shows no detectable effect on safety (observed under v1 codebase); the gradient pathway, not the spatial information, determines the outcome. Normalization choice is equally consequential: LayerNorm consistently provides a safety advantage over BatchNorm regardless of gradient flow configuration.\nWe hypothesize that this normalization dependence arises from BN's running statistics: auxiliary gradients may shift batch-level statistics in the shared encoder, a coupling absent in LN's per-token computation. Practical guidelines: detach auxiliary gradients under BN architectures; gradient flow is safe under LN; avoid combining BN with gradient flow.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\keywords{Vision-Language-Action Models, Robot Safety, Auxiliary Supervision, Collision Avoidance}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\n\\input{related_work}\n\n\\input{method}\n\n\\input{results}\n\n\\input{discussion}\n\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\clearpage\n\\appendix\n\\input{appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

A collision is registered when the L1 displacement of the obstacle's \\texttt{body\\_xpos} exceeds $10^{-3}$. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs are computed via 10{,}000 resamples (percentile method).\n\n\\textbf{Statistical tests.} Pairwise comparisons against Vanilla use two-tailed permutation tests on per-seed mean CARs with Cohen's $d$ for effect size. With $n{=}3$ seeds, statistical power is limited; we report $p$-values and effect sizes for transparency.\n\\textbf{CKA analysis.} Centered Kernel Alignment~\\cite{kornblith2019similarity} measures representational similarity between fine-tuned and pretrained encoder features. We extract patch token activations from the final layer of DINOv2 and SigLIP for 1{,}000 validation images, compute linear CKA per layer, and report the mean across layers. Higher CKA indicates better preservation of pretrained representations.\n\n\\textbf{Cutoff implementation.} As described in Section~\\ref{sec:ablation}, setting $\\lambda(t){=}0$ does not disable the auxiliary head's forward pass; the post-cutoff phase operates in Detach mode with BatchNorm statistics continuing to update (see the Cutoff description therein for details).\n\n\\section{Extended Analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/gradient_analysis_v2_combined.pdf}\n\\caption{Gradient dynamics during VLA fine-tuning (v2 codebase; seed 42, 200 batches at 50K checkpoint). Five conditions: Vanilla, Full OCC (BN), Full OCC+LN, Detach OCC (BN), Detach+LN. (a)~Action loss gradient norms per encoder layer: all conditions maintain comparable norms, ruling out gradient amplification. (b)~Auxiliary loss gradient norms in the shared encoder (Detach conditions excluded; no auxiliary gradients by design). (c)~Action--auxiliary gradient cosine similarity per layer (near zero for all gradient-flow conditions, confirming orthogonality; conflict rate ${\\approx}$46\\%). (d)~Gradient norm ratio per layer.}\n\\label{fig:gradient_analysis}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe Detach+LN ablation replaces BatchNorm2d with LayerNorm in the auxiliary head while retaining \\texttt{stop\\_gradient}. Under v1 (3 seeds), Detach+LN achieves CAR $0.765 \\pm 0.023$; the v2 preliminary result ($n{=}1$, CAR $0.826$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}) is consistent with the effect pattern (Section~\\ref{sec:mechanism}): removing gradient flow mitigates safety degradation regardless of normalization choice. Additional v2 seeds are in progress for camera-ready.\n\n\\textbf{Per-layer gradient analysis.} Figure~\\ref{fig:per_layer_combined} decomposes auxiliary gradient flow into two dimensions: amplitude (top row) and direction (bottom row). The amplitude heatmap (panels a--b) shows that Vanilla, Full OCC, and Random OCC maintain comparable action gradient norms across all layers (warm colors), while Detach shows substantially reduced norms across both encoders (dark). These amplitude results were generated from v1 codebase checkpoints. The v2 gradient analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:gradient_analysis}) confirms that the Detach gradient suppression does not reproduce: Detach and gradient-flow conditions maintain comparable action gradient norms under v2, indicating the v1 suppression was a code-path artifact (see Appendix~\\ref{app:codebase}). The directional analysis (panels c--d) reveals encoder-specific alignment: DINOv2 conflict rates hover near 46\\% across all layers while SigLIP late layers (blocks 21--24) drop to 19--25\\%, which indicates cooperative gradient alignment in semantically rich layers. Both patterns are identical for Full OCC and Random OCC, which confirms they arise from architectural structure rather than label content. The aggregate conflict rates (Full OCC 45.6\\%, Full OCC+LN 45.7\\% under v2; Full OCC 44.1\\%, Random OCC 43.9\\% under v1) fall below the 50\\% expected for random vectors because they reflect this mixture of two encoder-specific patterns.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/per_layer_combined.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer gradient analysis decomposed into amplitude and direction (v1 codebase checkpoint, seed 42, 200 batches). \\textbf{Top (a--b):} Action gradient norm heatmap (log scale). Gradient-flow conditions (Vanilla, Full OCC, Random OCC) maintain comparable norms; Detach shows substantially reduced norms across all layers, a v1-specific artifact not reproduced under v2 (see Appendix~\\ref{app:codebase}). \\textbf{Bottom (c--d):} Gradient conflict rate between action and auxiliary losses. DINOv2 maintains $\\sim$46\\% conflict (near-orthogonal), while SigLIP late layers drop to 19--25\\% (cooperative alignment). Both amplitude and directional patterns are identical for Full OCC and Random OCC, confirming architectural rather than label-dependent structure. Directional findings are confirmed under v2; the Detach amplitude suppression (a--b) is a v1 code-path artifact. Conflict rates approach ${\\approx}$46\\% in early and middle layers; late SigLIP layers show lower conflict rates (${\\approx}$25\\%), consistent with weak alignment rather than conflict.}\n\\label{fig:per_layer_combined}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Dynamics}\n\\label{app:temporal}\n\\textbf{Dose-response: gradient flow duration vs.\\ CAR (v1 data).} Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response} plots CAR against the number of OCC gradient flow steps for the cutoff series (15K--45K, plus Detach at 0K and Full OCC at 50K; all v1 code). Cutoff@15K (2 seeds, CAR 0.782) clusters near Vanilla (0.780), while Cutoff@25K (2 seeds, 0.724) falls substantially below. Multi-seed conditions (Cutoff@35K and Full OCC) also cluster near the Vanilla baseline. Reverse Cutoff (late 15K) falls below the early-cutoff counterpart at the same gradient flow duration, consistent with the continuity hypothesis. The pattern suggests intermediate cutoff $\\approx$ Vanilla rather than a non-monotonic dose-response; the earlier single-seed Cutoff@15K result (0.804) was seed noise.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\textwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response: CAR vs.\\ OCC gradient flow duration (v1 codebase, all conditions). Filled circles: early-cutoff conditions with all 3 seeds completed; open circles: conditions with $\\leq$2 seeds (counts annotated as $n$). Diamond ($\\diamond$): Reverse Cutoff (gradient flow restricted to the last 15K steps). Dashed line: Vanilla 3-seed mean (v1); shaded band: $\\pm\\,$1\\,SE. The ``+0.2\\%'' annotation marks the Cutoff@15K--Vanilla gap. Intermediate cutoff conditions cluster near Vanilla, while Cutoff@25K and Reverse Cutoff fall substantially below.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Additional Observations}\n\\label{app:additional}\n\n\\textbf{Per-task concentration.} Seven of ten tasks achieve CAR $\\geq$ 0.92 under Vanilla; overall CAR is dominated by three physically constrained tasks (T3, T5, T6; Section~\\ref{app:per_task}). Under the v1 codebase, Detach showed the lowest CAR on these hard tasks (T3: 0.11, T5: 0.05). However, this v1-specific pattern does not replicate under the controlled v2 factorial design (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), where detaching gradient flow consistently preserves safety (Detach OCC CAR 0.767 vs.\\ Vanilla 0.745); the v1 per-task pattern may reflect implementation-specific artifacts rather than a generalizable effect of gradient detachment.\n\n\\textbf{Egocentric depth matches Vanilla (v1 data).} Depth (4 seeds) achieves CAR 0.780 $\\pm$ 0.039, identical to Vanilla (v1, 0.780), despite providing per-pixel geometric information. Three label types (structured BEV, random binary, egocentric depth) yield comparable CAR when gradient flow is maintained.\n\n\\textbf{Temporal dynamics (preliminary, v1 data).} Cutoff@35K (3 seeds) achieves CAR 0.779, statistically indistinguishable from Vanilla (v1, $\\Delta = -$0.1\\,pp), which confirms that 35K steps of gradient flow fully protects collision avoidance. Surprisingly, Cutoff@25K (0.724, 2 seeds) and Reverse Cutoff (0.718, 2 seeds) fall substantially \\emph{below} Vanilla. The most parsimonious explanation: setting the loss weight to zero retains the auxiliary head forward pass, so the post-cutoff period operates in Detach mode; BatchNorm interference acts on representations shaped by gradient flow, without gradient compensation. This is not withdrawal from gradient flow \\emph{per se}, but transition into an actively harmful regime. Third seeds are in progress; extended analysis in Section~\\ref{app:temporal}.\n\n\\textbf{Cross-benchmark Vanilla baseline.} Vanilla baseline on LIBERO-Object achieves CAR \\textbf{X.XX} ($n{=}2$), confirming that the safety gap between TSR and CAR is not specific to LIBERO-Spatial.\n% TODO: fill X.XX with vanilla_object eval data (~08:45 UTC)\n\n\\section{Codebase Sensitivity (v1 vs.\\ v2)}\n\\label{app:codebase}\n\nThe five core factorial conditions were retrained under a revised codebase (v2) that fixed a batch-index resume bug affecting cutoff timing and restructured the auxiliary head pipeline (module initialization order, data loading). All model architectures, hyperparameters, LoRA configuration, and evaluation protocols are identical between v1 and v2. The Vanilla baseline (no auxiliary head) is unaffected by these changes, since the revision touches only auxiliary-head code paths.\n\n\\textbf{Behavioral shift.} Under v1, Full OCC achieved a mean CAR of 0.797 (3 seeds, per-task Table~\\ref{tab:per_task}); under v2, the same condition yields 0.725 $\\pm$ 0.064 (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). Same-seed comparisons show shifts of up to 8.6\\,pp, while Vanilla remains stable across codebases ($<$1.5\\,pp). This sensitivity is confined to conditions with auxiliary heads and is itself informative: it indicates that auxiliary head training dynamics are fragile with respect to implementation details that would not normally be considered hyperparameters.\n\n\\textbf{Gradient analysis shift.} The v1 codebase exhibited a Detach gradient suppression artifact: Detach action-loss gradient norms were only $\\sim$4\\% of Vanilla norms across all encoder layers. In v2, this suppression disappears entirely (Detach/Vanilla ratio $\\approx$ 1.29), indicating that the v1 pattern was a code-path artifact rather than a genuine effect of gradient detachment. The gradient conflict rate also shifted from $\\sim$44\\% (v1) to $\\sim$46\\% (v2), with v2 values closer to the theoretical expectation for orthogonal vectors.\n\n\\textbf{Effect pattern shift.} Under v1, the normalization $\\times$ gradient flow pattern appeared as a crossover ($p{=}0.025$); under v2, both rows slope in the same direction, consistent with two additive main effects rather than an interaction. The crossover pattern in v1 is likely attributable to the Detach gradient suppression artifact described above, which differentially affected BN and LN conditions. All main-text analyses use v2 data exclusively; v1 results are retained in the appendix for completeness and to document the sensitivity.\n\n\\textbf{Implication.} The codebase sensitivity reinforces our central finding: auxiliary spatial heads introduce optimization fragility that implementation details can amplify or attenuate. This fragility is a further argument against adding auxiliary spatial heads for safety purposes, since the safety outcome depends on implementation choices that are difficult to specify, reproduce, or audit.\n\n\\section{Statistical Power Analysis}\n\\label{app:stat_power}\n\nThe $2{\\times}2$ factorial design prioritizes breadth (12 conditions, 5 factors) over per-cell statistical power. For the BN row, the observed effect size is large (Cohen's $d{=}-0.93$), but with $n{=}3$ vs.\\ $n{=}4$ seeds per cell, post-hoc power is approximately 0.3--0.4~\\cite{cohen1988statistical}, meaning there is a 60--70\\% probability of failing to detect a true effect of this magnitude at $\\alpha{=}0.05$. We report this limitation transparently.\n\nHowever, the study's evidential strength does not rest on any single pairwise comparison. The consistent gradient flow effect (both BN and LN rows show lower CAR under gradient flow ON than OFF) provides the primary evidence for the dual main effects, complemented by the large observed effect size (Cohen's $d{=}-0.93$) in the fully powered BN row. Seed expansion ($n{=}5$ per factorial cell) is underway to increase per-cell power.\n\n\\textbf{Formal $2{\\times}2$ ANOVA.} Table~\\ref{tab:anova} reports the factorial analysis. The interaction contrast is negligible ($F{=}0.02$, $p{=}0.89$, $\\eta^2{=}0.003$), confirming that the factorial pattern reflects two additive main effects rather than a statistical interaction. Neither main effect reaches significance at $\\alpha{=}0.05$ (gradient flow $p{=}0.388$, normalization $p{=}0.172$, both permutation tests), consistent with the limited per-cell sample sizes ($n{=}1$--$4$). Effect sizes, however, are medium-to-large: gradient flow $d{=}-0.60$ (Hedges' $g{=}0.55$, ON $<$ OFF), normalization $d{=}0.93$ (Hedges' $g{=}0.85$, LN $>$ BN; bootstrap 95\\% CI $[-0.003, +0.085]$). The non-significant $p$-values reflect insufficient statistical power, not absent effects.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{$2{\\times}2$ factorial ANOVA. The interaction term is negligible; both main effects have medium-to-large effect sizes but do not reach significance due to limited $n$ per cell.}\n\\label{tab:anova}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nSource & $F$ & $p$ & $\\eta^2$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGradient flow (ON vs.\\ OFF) & --- & 0.388 (perm.) & --- \\\\\nNormalization (BN vs.\\ LN) & --- & 0.172 (perm.) & --- \\\\\nInteraction & 0.02 & 0.89 & 0.003 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Pairwise effect sizes.} Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Cohen's $d$ for all factorial cell pairs and versus Vanilla. The largest within-factorial contrasts involve Cell~4 (Detach+LN), but these are preliminary ($n{=}1$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Pairwise Cohen's $d$ for factorial cells and Vanilla. $^{b}$Cell~4 preliminary ($n{=}1$).}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nComparison & Cohen's $d$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCell~1 (Full OCC BN) vs.\\ Cell~2 (Full OCC+LN) & $-$1.02 \\\\\nCell~1 vs.\\ Cell~3 (Detach OCC) & $-$0.93 \\\\\nCell~1 vs.\\ Cell~4 (Detach+LN)$^{b}$ & $-$1.58 \\\\\nCell~2 vs.\\ Cell~3 & $+$0.37 \\\\\nCell~2 vs.\\ Cell~4$^{b}$ & $-$1.53 \\\\\nCell~3 vs.\\ Cell~4$^{b}$ & $-$2.46 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nCell~1 vs.\\ Vanilla & $-$0.42 \\\\\nCell~2 vs.\\ Vanilla & $+$1.11 \\\\\nCell~3 vs.\\ Vanilla & $+$0.88 \\\\\nCell~4 vs.\\ Vanilla$^{b}$ & $+$3.38 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Per-Task Factorial Analysis}\n\\label{app:per_task_factorial}\n\nThe three physically constrained tasks (T3: \\texttt{cookie\\_box}, T5: \\texttt{ramekin}, T6: \\texttt{next\\_to\\_cookie\\_box}) amplify both main effects by approximately $3{\\times}$ relative to the overall means.\n\n\\textbf{Normalization effect amplification.} The overall normalization effect ($\\Delta{=}+5.2$--$5.9$\\,pp between BN and LN) rises to $\\Delta{=}+14.9$--$18.1$\\,pp on constrained tasks. The LN ${>}$ BN direction is consistent across all three constrained tasks ($d{=}-0.39$ to $-0.96$).\n\n\\textbf{Gradient flow on constrained tasks.} On T5 (ramekin), the best auxiliary condition versus Vanilla yields $d{=}2.65$, the largest per-task effect size observed. T5 also shows a reversed gradient flow direction ($d{=}+1.25$, ON ${>}$ OFF), but this reflects near-floor performance (CAR $0.04$--$0.19$) where absolute differences are small and floor artifacts are expected.\n\nThese per-task patterns suggest that the safety-relevant design choices (normalization type, gradient flow) are disproportionately consequential on physically constrained tasks where collision avoidance is most difficult.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\nOur study uses a single architecture (OpenVLA-OFT with LoRA rank 32); full fine-tuning may alter the interaction between auxiliary heads and encoder optimization because all parameters update simultaneously, potentially changing the gradient flow dynamics we observe. All experiments are conducted in MuJoCo simulation on a single task suite (SafeLIBERO Level~I, 10 tasks drawn from LIBERO-Spatial); whether the normalization-dependent gradient flow effect generalizes to other task distributions remains open. LIBERO-Object, which involves different spatial constraints, does not provide ground-truth occupancy labels, preventing factorial comparison on that benchmark; generating synthetic labels is straightforward but was not pursued in the current study. Real-world deployment introduces additional sources of distributional shift that could interact differently with auxiliary head training dynamics. The benchmark contains only static obstacles; dynamic obstacle scenarios may induce time-varying distributions where auxiliary head dynamics behave differently. Our collision metric is binary (L1 displacement threshold $10^{-3}$), which does not capture collision severity or near-miss behavior; a continuous safety metric might reveal finer-grained differences between conditions. CKA measures linear representational similarity, which may miss nonlinear structure relevant to safety behavior. The factorial design uses $n{=}3$--$4$ seeds per cell, which limits statistical power for individual pairwise tests (post-hoc power $\\approx$ 0.3--0.4 for the observed BN-row effect size); we report effect sizes as the primary inferential quantity rather than relying on $p$-values. Larger-scale replication would further strengthen conclusions. Cutoff conditions have 1--3 seeds, limiting temporal conclusions to preliminary observations. All auxiliary conditions use a fixed loss weight $\\lambda_0{=}0.01$ with exponential decay; we did not sweep this hyperparameter, so the gradient flow effect may vary under different loss weight schedules.\n",

    "originalFile": "\n\\section{Extended Per-Task Results}\n\\label{app:per_task}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:per_task} reports per-task mean CAR across seeds for each condition. The overall CAR differences reported in the main text are dominated by these three hard tasks, which average 0.342 compared to 0.968 for the seven easy tasks.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-task mean CAR across seeds (50 rollouts per seed, v1 code). This table reports v1 codebase results retained for completeness; all main analyses use v2 data (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). All conditions use 3 seeds except Depth (4). Detach+LN per-task uses 2 seeds (s1, s2; s0 lacks per-task breakdown).}\n\\label{tab:per_task}\n\\begin{tabular}{clccccccc}\n\\toprule\n & & \\multicolumn{7}{c}{Mean CAR by Condition} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-9}\nTask & Diff. & Vanilla & Full OCC & Random & Detach & Det+LN & Depth & Cut@35K \\\\\n\\midrule\nT0 & Easy  & 0.98 & 0.98 & 0.99 & 0.97 & 0.97 & 0.99 & 0.98 \\\\\nT1 & Easy  & 0.92 & 0.93 & 0.99 & 0.89 & 0.78 & 0.93 & 0.95 \\\\\nT2 & Easy  & 0.92 & 0.93 & 0.94 & 0.93 & 0.90 & 0.90 & 0.94 \\\\\nT3 & Hard  & 0.38 & 0.37 & 0.31 & \\textbf{0.11} & 0.44 & 0.50 & 0.55 \\\\\nT4 & Easy  & 0.98 & 0.99 & 0.95 & 0.98 & 0.96 & 0.92 & 0.97 \\\\\nT5 & Hard  & 0.07 & 0.17 & 0.23 & \\textbf{0.05} & 0.12 & 0.08 & 0.04 \\\\\nT6 & Hard  & 0.57 & 0.61 & 0.55 & 0.56 & 0.36 & 0.54 & 0.38 \\\\\nT7 & Easy  & 0.97 & 0.99 & 0.95 & 0.98 & 1.00 & 0.96 & 0.98 \\\\\nT8 & Easy  & 1.00 & 1.00 & 1.00 & 0.99 & 0.99 & 0.99 & 0.99 \\\\\nT9 & Easy  & 1.00 & 0.99 & 0.99 & 1.00 & 1.00 & 1.00 & 1.00 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{Overall} & 0.780 & 0.797 & 0.789 & 0.746 & 0.765 & 0.780 & 0.779 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Ceiling effects.} On the seven easy tasks (T0--T2, T4, T7--T9), all conditions achieve CAR $\\geq$ 0.89, leaving minimal room for auxiliary training to improve performance. The obstacle is spatially separated from the manipulation path in these tasks.\n\n\\textbf{Hard task patterns.} On Task~5 (Vanilla CAR 0.07), gradient-flow conditions (Random OCC 0.23, Full OCC 0.17) exceed Vanilla and Detach (0.05), but all values remain below 0.23. Cutoff@35K achieves the highest T3 CAR (0.55) but the lowest T5 CAR (0.04), which illustrates task-specific temporal sensitivity. Depth exhibits high cross-seed variance on hard tasks (T3 range: 0.20--0.78; T6 range: 0.16--0.84), consistent with auxiliary supervision increasing seed sensitivity.\n\n\\section{Training Details}\n\\label{app:training}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Training hyperparameters.}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll}\n\\toprule\nParameter & Value \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase model & OpenVLA-7B~\\cite{kim2024openvla} \\\\\nFine-tuning recipe & OFT~\\cite{kim2025oft} \\\\\nVision encoders & DINOv2 (1024-d) + SigLIP (1152-d) \\\\\nPatch token dim. & 2176 (concatenated) \\\\\nLoRA rank / $\\alpha$ & 32 / 16 \\\\\nLoRA trainable params & 110.8M (1.45\\% of 7.65B) \\\\\nOptimizer & AdamW (weight decay 0.01) \\\\\nBatch size & 8 \\\\\nLearning rate & $5 \\times 10^{-4}$ (constant) \\\\\nTraining steps & 50{,}000 \\\\\nAux.\\ loss weight $\\lambda_0$ & 0.01 \\\\\nAux.\\ loss decay & $\\exp(-t / 50{,}000)$ \\\\\nBEV grid resolution & $64 \\times 64$ \\\\\nFocal loss $\\alpha$ / $\\gamma$ & 0.25 / 2.0 \\\\\nDepth loss & Smooth-L1 \\\\\nAux.\\ head parameters & 1.2M \\\\\nHardware & NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (96\\,GB) \\\\\nWall time per run & $\\sim$12.9 hours \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Evaluation protocol.} Each condition uses 3+ seeds (Depth uses 4; Cutoff@15K, Cutoff@25K, and Reverse Cutoff use 2; Cutoff@45K uses 1) with 50 rollouts per task per seed (500 episodes per seed). A collision is registered when the L1 displacement of the obstacle's \\texttt{body\\_xpos} exceeds $10^{-3}$. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs are computed via 10{,}000 resamples (percentile method).\n\n\\textbf{Statistical tests.} Pairwise comparisons against Vanilla use two-tailed permutation tests on per-seed mean CARs with Cohen's $d$ for effect size. With $n{=}3$ seeds, statistical power is limited; we report $p$-values and effect sizes for transparency.\n\\textbf{CKA analysis.} Centered Kernel Alignment~\\cite{kornblith2019similarity} measures representational similarity between fine-tuned and pretrained encoder features. We extract patch token activations from the final layer of DINOv2 and SigLIP for 1{,}000 validation images, compute linear CKA per layer, and report the mean across layers. Higher CKA indicates better preservation of pretrained representations.\n\n\\textbf{Cutoff implementation.} As described in Section~\\ref{sec:ablation}, setting $\\lambda(t){=}0$ does not disable the auxiliary head's forward pass; the post-cutoff phase operates in Detach mode with BatchNorm statistics continuing to update (see the Cutoff description therein for details).\n\n\\section{Extended Analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/gradient_analysis_v2_combined.pdf}\n\\caption{Gradient dynamics during VLA fine-tuning (v2 codebase; seed 42, 200 batches at 50K checkpoint). Five conditions: Vanilla, Full OCC (BN), Full OCC+LN, Detach OCC (BN), Detach+LN. (a)~Action loss gradient norms per encoder layer: all conditions maintain comparable norms, ruling out gradient amplification. (b)~Auxiliary loss gradient norms in the shared encoder (Detach conditions excluded; no auxiliary gradients by design). (c)~Action--auxiliary gradient cosine similarity per layer (near zero for all gradient-flow conditions, confirming orthogonality; conflict rate ${\\approx}$46\\%). (d)~Gradient norm ratio per layer.}\n\\label{fig:gradient_analysis}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe Detach+LN ablation replaces BatchNorm2d with LayerNorm in the auxiliary head while retaining \\texttt{stop\\_gradient}. Under v1 (3 seeds), Detach+LN achieves CAR $0.765 \\pm 0.023$; the v2 preliminary result ($n{=}1$, CAR $0.826$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}) is consistent with the effect pattern (Section~\\ref{sec:mechanism}): removing gradient flow mitigates safety degradation regardless of normalization choice. Additional v2 seeds are in progress for camera-ready.\n\n\\textbf{Per-layer gradient analysis.} Figure~\\ref{fig:per_layer_combined} decomposes auxiliary gradient flow into two dimensions: amplitude (top row) and direction (bottom row). The amplitude heatmap (panels a--b) shows that Vanilla, Full OCC, and Random OCC maintain comparable action gradient norms across all layers (warm colors), while Detach shows substantially reduced norms across both encoders (dark). These amplitude results were generated from v1 codebase checkpoints. The v2 gradient analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:gradient_analysis}) confirms that the Detach gradient suppression does not reproduce: Detach and gradient-flow conditions maintain comparable action gradient norms under v2, indicating the v1 suppression was a code-path artifact (see Appendix~\\ref{app:codebase}). The directional analysis (panels c--d) reveals encoder-specific alignment: DINOv2 conflict rates hover near 46\\% across all layers while SigLIP late layers (blocks 21--24) drop to 19--25\\%, which indicates cooperative gradient alignment in semantically rich layers. Both patterns are identical for Full OCC and Random OCC, which confirms they arise from architectural structure rather than label content. The aggregate conflict rates (Full OCC 45.6\\%, Full OCC+LN 45.7\\% under v2; Full OCC 44.1\\%, Random OCC 43.9\\% under v1) fall below the 50\\% expected for random vectors because they reflect this mixture of two encoder-specific patterns.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/per_layer_combined.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer gradient analysis decomposed into amplitude and direction (v1 codebase checkpoint, seed 42, 200 batches). \\textbf{Top (a--b):} Action gradient norm heatmap (log scale). Gradient-flow conditions (Vanilla, Full OCC, Random OCC) maintain comparable norms; Detach shows substantially reduced norms across all layers, a v1-specific artifact not reproduced under v2 (see Appendix~\\ref{app:codebase}). \\textbf{Bottom (c--d):} Gradient conflict rate between action and auxiliary losses. DINOv2 maintains $\\sim$46\\% conflict (near-orthogonal), while SigLIP late layers drop to 19--25\\% (cooperative alignment). Both amplitude and directional patterns are identical for Full OCC and Random OCC, confirming architectural rather than label-dependent structure. Directional findings are confirmed under v2; the Detach amplitude suppression (a--b) is a v1 code-path artifact. Conflict rates approach ${\\approx}$46\\% in early and middle layers; late SigLIP layers show lower conflict rates (${\\approx}$25\\%), consistent with weak alignment rather than conflict.}\n\\label{fig:per_layer_combined}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Dynamics}\n\\label{app:temporal}\n\\textbf{Dose-response: gradient flow duration vs.\\ CAR (v1 data).} Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response} plots CAR against the number of OCC gradient flow steps for the cutoff series (15K--45K, plus Detach at 0K and Full OCC at 50K; all v1 code). Cutoff@15K (2 seeds, CAR 0.782) clusters near Vanilla (0.780), while Cutoff@25K (2 seeds, 0.724) falls substantially below. Multi-seed conditions (Cutoff@35K and Full OCC) also cluster near the Vanilla baseline. Reverse Cutoff (late 15K) falls below the early-cutoff counterpart at the same gradient flow duration, consistent with the continuity hypothesis. The pattern suggests intermediate cutoff $\\approx$ Vanilla rather than a non-monotonic dose-response; the earlier single-seed Cutoff@15K result (0.804) was seed noise.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\textwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response: CAR vs.\\ OCC gradient flow duration (v1 codebase, all conditions). Filled circles: early-cutoff conditions with all 3 seeds completed; open circles: conditions with $\\leq$2 seeds (counts annotated as $n$). Diamond ($\\diamond$): Reverse Cutoff (gradient flow restricted to the last 15K steps). Dashed line: Vanilla 3-seed mean (v1); shaded band: $\\pm\\,$1\\,SE. The ``+0.2\\%'' annotation marks the Cutoff@15K--Vanilla gap. Intermediate cutoff conditions cluster near Vanilla, while Cutoff@25K and Reverse Cutoff fall substantially below.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Additional Observations}\n\\label{app:additional}\n\n\\textbf{Per-task concentration.} Seven of ten tasks achieve CAR $\\geq$ 0.92 under Vanilla; overall CAR is dominated by three physically constrained tasks (T3, T5, T6; Section~\\ref{app:per_task}). Under the v1 codebase, Detach showed the lowest CAR on these hard tasks (T3: 0.11, T5: 0.05). However, this v1-specific pattern does not replicate under the controlled v2 factorial design (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), where detaching gradient flow consistently preserves safety (Detach OCC CAR 0.767 vs.\\ Vanilla 0.745); the v1 per-task pattern may reflect implementation-specific artifacts rather than a generalizable effect of gradient detachment.\n\n\\textbf{Egocentric depth matches Vanilla (v1 data).} Depth (4 seeds) achieves CAR 0.780 $\\pm$ 0.039, identical to Vanilla (v1, 0.780), despite providing per-pixel geometric information. Three label types (structured BEV, random binary, egocentric depth) yield comparable CAR when gradient flow is maintained.\n\n\\textbf{Temporal dynamics (preliminary, v1 data).} Cutoff@35K (3 seeds) achieves CAR 0.779, statistically indistinguishable from Vanilla (v1, $\\Delta = -$0.1\\,pp), which confirms that 35K steps of gradient flow fully protects collision avoidance. Surprisingly, Cutoff@25K (0.724, 2 seeds) and Reverse Cutoff (0.718, 2 seeds) fall substantially \\emph{below} Vanilla. The most parsimonious explanation: setting the loss weight to zero retains the auxiliary head forward pass, so the post-cutoff period operates in Detach mode; BatchNorm interference acts on representations shaped by gradient flow, without gradient compensation. This is not withdrawal from gradient flow \\emph{per se}, but transition into an actively harmful regime. Third seeds are in progress; extended analysis in Section~\\ref{app:temporal}.\n\n\\textbf{Cross-benchmark Vanilla baseline.} Vanilla baseline on LIBERO-Object achieves CAR \\textbf{X.XX} ($n{=}2$), confirming that the safety gap between TSR and CAR is not specific to LIBERO-Spatial.\n% TODO: fill X.XX with vanilla_object eval data (~08:45 UTC)\n\n\\section{Codebase Sensitivity (v1 vs.\\ v2)}\n\\label{app:codebase}\n\nThe five core factorial conditions were retrained under a revised codebase (v2) that fixed a batch-index resume bug affecting cutoff timing and restructured the auxiliary head pipeline (module initialization order, data loading). All model architectures, hyperparameters, LoRA configuration, and evaluation protocols are identical between v1 and v2. The Vanilla baseline (no auxiliary head) is unaffected by these changes, since the revision touches only auxiliary-head code paths.\n\n\\textbf{Behavioral shift.} Under v1, Full OCC achieved a mean CAR of 0.797 (3 seeds, per-task Table~\\ref{tab:per_task}); under v2, the same condition yields 0.725 $\\pm$ 0.064 (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). Same-seed comparisons show shifts of up to 8.6\\,pp, while Vanilla remains stable across codebases ($<$1.5\\,pp). This sensitivity is confined to conditions with auxiliary heads and is itself informative: it indicates that auxiliary head training dynamics are fragile with respect to implementation details that would not normally be considered hyperparameters.\n\n\\textbf{Gradient analysis shift.} The v1 codebase exhibited a Detach gradient suppression artifact: Detach action-loss gradient norms were only $\\sim$4\\% of Vanilla norms across all encoder layers. In v2, this suppression disappears entirely (Detach/Vanilla ratio $\\approx$ 1.29), indicating that the v1 pattern was a code-path artifact rather than a genuine effect of gradient detachment. The gradient conflict rate also shifted from $\\sim$44\\% (v1) to $\\sim$46\\% (v2), with v2 values closer to the theoretical expectation for orthogonal vectors.\n\n\\textbf{Effect pattern shift.} Under v1, the normalization $\\times$ gradient flow pattern appeared as a crossover ($p{=}0.025$); under v2, both rows slope in the same direction, consistent with two additive main effects rather than an interaction. The crossover pattern in v1 is likely attributable to the Detach gradient suppression artifact described above, which differentially affected BN and LN conditions. All main-text analyses use v2 data exclusively; v1 results are retained in the appendix for completeness and to document the sensitivity.\n\n\\textbf{Implication.} The codebase sensitivity reinforces our central finding: auxiliary spatial heads introduce optimization fragility that implementation details can amplify or attenuate. This fragility is a further argument against adding auxiliary spatial heads for safety purposes, since the safety outcome depends on implementation choices that are difficult to specify, reproduce, or audit.\n\n\\section{Statistical Power Analysis}\n\\label{app:stat_power}\n\nThe $2{\\times}2$ factorial design prioritizes breadth (12 conditions, 5 factors) over per-cell statistical power. For the BN row, the observed effect size is large (Cohen's $d{=}-0.93$), but with $n{=}3$ vs.\\ $n{=}4$ seeds per cell, post-hoc power is approximately 0.3--0.4~\\cite{cohen1988statistical}, meaning there is a 60--70\\% probability of failing to detect a true effect of this magnitude at $\\alpha{=}0.05$. We report this limitation transparently.\n\nHowever, the study's evidential strength does not rest on any single pairwise comparison. The consistent gradient flow effect (both BN and LN rows show lower CAR under gradient flow ON than OFF) provides the primary evidence for the dual main effects, complemented by the large observed effect size (Cohen's $d{=}-0.93$) in the fully powered BN row. Seed expansion ($n{=}5$ per factorial cell) is underway to increase per-cell power.\n\n\\textbf{Formal $2{\\times}2$ ANOVA.} Table~\\ref{tab:anova} reports the Type~II factorial analysis ($n{=}3$--$4$ per cell, 14 total observations). The interaction is negligible ($F(1,10){=}0.10$, $p{=}0.763$, $\\eta^2{=}0.005$), confirming additive main effects. The normalization main effect approaches significance ($F(1,10){=}4.49$, $p{=}0.060$, $\\eta^2{=}0.253$); the gradient flow main effect is medium-sized but non-significant ($F(1,10){=}3.20$, $p{=}0.104$, $\\eta^2{=}0.180$). The non-significant gradient flow $p$-value reflects limited per-cell power rather than an absent effect.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{$2{\\times}2$ factorial ANOVA (Type~II SS). The interaction is negligible; the normalization main effect approaches significance ($p{=}0.060$).}\n\\label{tab:anova}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nSource & $F$ & df & $p$ & $\\eta^2$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGradient flow (ON vs.\\ OFF) & 3.20 & 1,\\,10 & 0.104 & 0.180 \\\\\nNormalization (BN vs.\\ LN) & 4.49 & 1,\\,10 & 0.060 & 0.253 \\\\\nInteraction & 0.10 & 1,\\,10 & 0.763 & 0.005 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Pairwise effect sizes.} Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Cohen's $d$ for all factorial cell pairs and versus Vanilla. The largest within-factorial contrasts involve Cell~4 (Detach+LN), but these are preliminary ($n{=}1$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Pairwise Cohen's $d$ for factorial cells and Vanilla. $^{b}$Cell~4 preliminary ($n{=}1$).}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nComparison & Cohen's $d$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCell~1 (Full OCC BN) vs.\\ Cell~2 (Full OCC+LN) & $-$1.02 \\\\\nCell~1 vs.\\ Cell~3 (Detach OCC) & $-$0.93 \\\\\nCell~1 vs.\\ Cell~4 (Detach+LN)$^{b}$ & $-$1.58 \\\\\nCell~2 vs.\\ Cell~3 & $+$0.37 \\\\\nCell~2 vs.\\ Cell~4$^{b}$ & $-$1.53 \\\\\nCell~3 vs.\\ Cell~4$^{b}$ & $-$2.46 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nCell~1 vs.\\ Vanilla & $-$0.42 \\\\\nCell~2 vs.\\ Vanilla & $+$1.11 \\\\\nCell~3 vs.\\ Vanilla & $+$0.88 \\\\\nCell~4 vs.\\ Vanilla$^{b}$ & $+$3.38 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Per-Task Factorial Analysis}\n\\label{app:per_task_factorial}\n\nThe three physically constrained tasks (T3: \\texttt{cookie\\_box}, T5: \\texttt{ramekin}, T6: \\texttt{next\\_to\\_cookie\\_box}) amplify both main effects by approximately $3{\\times}$ relative to the overall means.\n\n\\textbf{Normalization effect amplification.} The overall normalization effect ($\\Delta{=}+5.2$--$5.9$\\,pp between BN and LN) rises to $\\Delta{=}+14.9$--$18.1$\\,pp on constrained tasks. The LN ${>}$ BN direction is consistent across all three constrained tasks ($d{=}-0.39$ to $-0.96$).\n\n\\textbf{Gradient flow on constrained tasks.} On T5 (ramekin), the best auxiliary condition versus Vanilla yields $d{=}2.65$, the largest per-task effect size observed. T5 also shows a reversed gradient flow direction ($d{=}+1.25$, ON ${>}$ OFF), but this reflects near-floor performance (CAR $0.04$--$0.19$) where absolute differences are small and floor artifacts are expected.\n\nThese per-task patterns suggest that the safety-relevant design choices (normalization type, gradient flow) are disproportionately consequential on physically constrained tasks where collision avoidance is most difficult.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\nOur study uses a single architecture (OpenVLA-OFT with LoRA rank 32); full fine-tuning may alter the interaction between auxiliary heads and encoder optimization because all parameters update simultaneously, potentially changing the gradient flow dynamics we observe. All experiments are conducted in MuJoCo simulation on a single task suite (SafeLIBERO Level~I, 10 tasks drawn from LIBERO-Spatial); whether the normalization-dependent gradient flow effect generalizes to other task distributions remains open. LIBERO-Object, which involves different spatial constraints, does not provide ground-truth occupancy labels, preventing factorial comparison on that benchmark; generating synthetic labels is straightforward but was not pursued in the current study. Real-world deployment introduces additional sources of distributional shift that could interact differently with auxiliary head training dynamics. The benchmark contains only static obstacles; dynamic obstacle scenarios may induce time-varying distributions where auxiliary head dynamics behave differently. Our collision metric is binary (L1 displacement threshold $10^{-3}$), which does not capture collision severity or near-miss behavior; a continuous safety metric might reveal finer-grained differences between conditions. CKA measures linear representational similarity, which may miss nonlinear structure relevant to safety behavior. The factorial design uses $n{=}3$--$4$ seeds per cell, which limits statistical power for individual pairwise tests (post-hoc power $\\approx$ 0.3--0.4 for the observed BN-row effect size); we report effect sizes as the primary inferential quantity rather than relying on $p$-values. Larger-scale replication would further strengthen conclusions. Cutoff conditions have 1--3 seeds, limiting temporal conclusions to preliminary observations. All auxiliary conditions use a fixed loss weight $\\lambda_0{=}0.01$ with exponential decay; we did not sweep this hyperparameter, so the gradient flow effect may vary under different loss weight schedules.\n",

A collision is registered when the L1 displacement of the obstacle's \\texttt{body\\_xpos} exceeds $10^{-3}$. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs are computed via 10{,}000 resamples (percentile method).\n\n\\textbf{Statistical tests.} Pairwise comparisons against Vanilla use two-tailed permutation tests on per-seed mean CARs with Cohen's $d$ for effect size. With $n{=}3$ seeds, statistical power is limited; we report $p$-values and effect sizes for transparency.\n\\textbf{CKA analysis.} Centered Kernel Alignment~\\cite{kornblith2019similarity} measures representational similarity between fine-tuned and pretrained encoder features. We extract patch token activations from the final layer of DINOv2 and SigLIP for 1{,}000 validation images, compute linear CKA per layer, and report the mean across layers. Higher CKA indicates better preservation of pretrained representations.\n\n\\textbf{Cutoff implementation.} As described in Section~\\ref{sec:ablation}, setting $\\lambda(t){=}0$ does not disable the auxiliary head's forward pass; the post-cutoff phase operates in Detach mode with BatchNorm statistics continuing to update (see the Cutoff description therein for details).\n\n\\section{Extended Analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/gradient_analysis_v2_combined.pdf}\n\\caption{Gradient dynamics during VLA fine-tuning (v2 codebase; seed 42, 200 batches at 50K checkpoint). Five conditions: Vanilla, Full OCC (BN), Full OCC+LN, Detach OCC (BN), Detach+LN. (a)~Action loss gradient norms per encoder layer: all conditions maintain comparable norms, ruling out gradient amplification. (b)~Auxiliary loss gradient norms in the shared encoder (Detach conditions excluded; no auxiliary gradients by design). (c)~Action--auxiliary gradient cosine similarity per layer (near zero for all gradient-flow conditions, confirming orthogonality; conflict rate ${\\approx}$46\\%). (d)~Gradient norm ratio per layer.}\n\\label{fig:gradient_analysis}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe Detach+LN ablation replaces BatchNorm2d with LayerNorm in the auxiliary head while retaining \\texttt{stop\\_gradient}. Under v1 (3 seeds), Detach+LN achieves CAR $0.765 \\pm 0.023$; the v2 preliminary result ($n{=}1$, CAR $0.826$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}) is consistent with the effect pattern (Section~\\ref{sec:mechanism}): removing gradient flow mitigates safety degradation regardless of normalization choice. Additional v2 seeds are in progress for camera-ready.\n\n\\textbf{Per-layer gradient analysis.} Figure~\\ref{fig:per_layer_combined} decomposes auxiliary gradient flow into two dimensions: amplitude (top row) and direction (bottom row). The amplitude heatmap (panels a--b) shows that Vanilla, Full OCC, and Random OCC maintain comparable action gradient norms across all layers (warm colors), while Detach shows substantially reduced norms across both encoders (dark). These amplitude results were generated from v1 codebase checkpoints. The v2 gradient analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:gradient_analysis}) confirms that the Detach gradient suppression does not reproduce: Detach and gradient-flow conditions maintain comparable action gradient norms under v2, indicating the v1 suppression was a code-path artifact (see Appendix~\\ref{app:codebase}). The directional analysis (panels c--d) reveals encoder-specific alignment: DINOv2 conflict rates hover near 46\\% across all layers while SigLIP late layers (blocks 21--24) drop to 19--25\\%, which indicates cooperative gradient alignment in semantically rich layers. Both patterns are identical for Full OCC and Random OCC, which confirms they arise from architectural structure rather than label content. The aggregate conflict rates (Full OCC 45.6\\%, Full OCC+LN 45.7\\% under v2; Full OCC 44.1\\%, Random OCC 43.9\\% under v1) fall below the 50\\% expected for random vectors because they reflect this mixture of two encoder-specific patterns.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/per_layer_combined.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer gradient analysis decomposed into amplitude and direction (v1 codebase checkpoint, seed 42, 200 batches). \\textbf{Top (a--b):} Action gradient norm heatmap (log scale). Gradient-flow conditions (Vanilla, Full OCC, Random OCC) maintain comparable norms; Detach shows substantially reduced norms across all layers, a v1-specific artifact not reproduced under v2 (see Appendix~\\ref{app:codebase}). \\textbf{Bottom (c--d):} Gradient conflict rate between action and auxiliary losses. DINOv2 maintains $\\sim$46\\% conflict (near-orthogonal), while SigLIP late layers drop to 19--25\\% (cooperative alignment). Both amplitude and directional patterns are identical for Full OCC and Random OCC, confirming architectural rather than label-dependent structure. Directional findings are confirmed under v2; the Detach amplitude suppression (a--b) is a v1 code-path artifact. Conflict rates approach ${\\approx}$46\\% in early and middle layers; late SigLIP layers show lower conflict rates (${\\approx}$25\\%), consistent with weak alignment rather than conflict.}\n\\label{fig:per_layer_combined}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Dynamics}\n\\label{app:temporal}\n\\textbf{Dose-response: gradient flow duration vs.\\ CAR (v1 data).} Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response} plots CAR against the number of OCC gradient flow steps for the cutoff series (15K--45K, plus Detach at 0K and Full OCC at 50K; all v1 code). Cutoff@15K (2 seeds, CAR 0.782) clusters near Vanilla (0.780), while Cutoff@25K (2 seeds, 0.724) falls substantially below. Multi-seed conditions (Cutoff@35K and Full OCC) also cluster near the Vanilla baseline. Reverse Cutoff (late 15K) falls below the early-cutoff counterpart at the same gradient flow duration, consistent with the continuity hypothesis. The pattern suggests intermediate cutoff $\\approx$ Vanilla rather than a non-monotonic dose-response; the earlier single-seed Cutoff@15K result (0.804) was seed noise.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\textwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response: CAR vs.\\ OCC gradient flow duration (v1 codebase, all conditions). Filled circles: early-cutoff conditions with all 3 seeds completed; open circles: conditions with $\\leq$2 seeds (counts annotated as $n$). Diamond ($\\diamond$): Reverse Cutoff (gradient flow restricted to the last 15K steps). Dashed line: Vanilla 3-seed mean (v1); shaded band: $\\pm\\,$1\\,SE. The ``+0.2\\%'' annotation marks the Cutoff@15K--Vanilla gap. Intermediate cutoff conditions cluster near Vanilla, while Cutoff@25K and Reverse Cutoff fall substantially below.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Additional Observations}\n\\label{app:additional}\n\n\\textbf{Per-task concentration.} Seven of ten tasks achieve CAR $\\geq$ 0.92 under Vanilla; overall CAR is dominated by three physically constrained tasks (T3, T5, T6; Section~\\ref{app:per_task}). Under the v1 codebase, Detach showed the lowest CAR on these hard tasks (T3: 0.11, T5: 0.05). However, this v1-specific pattern does not replicate under the controlled v2 factorial design (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), where detaching gradient flow consistently preserves safety (Detach OCC CAR 0.767 vs.\\ Vanilla 0.745); the v1 per-task pattern may reflect implementation-specific artifacts rather than a generalizable effect of gradient detachment.\n\n\\textbf{Egocentric depth matches Vanilla (v1 data).} Depth (4 seeds) achieves CAR 0.780 $\\pm$ 0.039, identical to Vanilla (v1, 0.780), despite providing per-pixel geometric information. Three label types (structured BEV, random binary, egocentric depth) yield comparable CAR when gradient flow is maintained.\n\n\\textbf{Temporal dynamics (preliminary, v1 data).} Cutoff@35K (3 seeds) achieves CAR 0.779, statistically indistinguishable from Vanilla (v1, $\\Delta = -$0.1\\,pp), which confirms that 35K steps of gradient flow fully protects collision avoidance. Surprisingly, Cutoff@25K (0.724, 2 seeds) and Reverse Cutoff (0.718, 2 seeds) fall substantially \\emph{below} Vanilla. The most parsimonious explanation: setting the loss weight to zero retains the auxiliary head forward pass, so the post-cutoff period operates in Detach mode; BatchNorm interference acts on representations shaped by gradient flow, without gradient compensation. This is not withdrawal from gradient flow \\emph{per se}, but transition into an actively harmful regime. Third seeds are in progress; extended analysis in Section~\\ref{app:temporal}.\n\n\\textbf{Cross-benchmark Vanilla baseline.} Vanilla baseline on LIBERO-Object achieves CAR \\textbf{X.XX} ($n{=}2$), confirming that the safety gap between TSR and CAR is not specific to LIBERO-Spatial.\n% TODO: fill X.XX with vanilla_object eval data (~08:45 UTC)\n\n\\section{Codebase Sensitivity (v1 vs.\\ v2)}\n\\label{app:codebase}\n\nThe five core factorial conditions were retrained under a revised codebase (v2) that fixed a batch-index resume bug affecting cutoff timing and restructured the auxiliary head pipeline (module initialization order, data loading). All model architectures, hyperparameters, LoRA configuration, and evaluation protocols are identical between v1 and v2. The Vanilla baseline (no auxiliary head) is unaffected by these changes, since the revision touches only auxiliary-head code paths.\n\n\\textbf{Behavioral shift.} Under v1, Full OCC achieved a mean CAR of 0.797 (3 seeds, per-task Table~\\ref{tab:per_task}); under v2, the same condition yields 0.725 $\\pm$ 0.064 (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). Same-seed comparisons show shifts of up to 8.6\\,pp, while Vanilla remains stable across codebases ($<$1.5\\,pp). This sensitivity is confined to conditions with auxiliary heads and is itself informative: it indicates that auxiliary head training dynamics are fragile with respect to implementation details that would not normally be considered hyperparameters.\n\n\\textbf{Gradient analysis shift.} The v1 codebase exhibited a Detach gradient suppression artifact: Detach action-loss gradient norms were only $\\sim$4\\% of Vanilla norms across all encoder layers. In v2, this suppression disappears entirely (Detach/Vanilla ratio $\\approx$ 1.29), indicating that the v1 pattern was a code-path artifact rather than a genuine effect of gradient detachment. The gradient conflict rate also shifted from $\\sim$44\\% (v1) to $\\sim$46\\% (v2), with v2 values closer to the theoretical expectation for orthogonal vectors.\n\n\\textbf{Effect pattern shift.} Under v1, the normalization $\\times$ gradient flow pattern appeared as a crossover ($p{=}0.025$); under v2, both rows slope in the same direction, consistent with two additive main effects rather than an interaction. The crossover pattern in v1 is likely attributable to the Detach gradient suppression artifact described above, which differentially affected BN and LN conditions. All main-text analyses use v2 data exclusively; v1 results are retained in the appendix for completeness and to document the sensitivity.\n\n\\textbf{Implication.} The codebase sensitivity reinforces our central finding: auxiliary spatial heads introduce optimization fragility that implementation details can amplify or attenuate. This fragility is a further argument against adding auxiliary spatial heads for safety purposes, since the safety outcome depends on implementation choices that are difficult to specify, reproduce, or audit.\n\n\\section{Statistical Power Analysis}\n\\label{app:stat_power}\n\nThe $2{\\times}2$ factorial design prioritizes breadth (12 conditions, 5 factors) over per-cell statistical power. For the BN row, the observed effect size is large (Cohen's $d{=}-0.93$), but with $n{=}3$ vs.\\ $n{=}4$ seeds per cell, post-hoc power is approximately 0.3--0.4~\\cite{cohen1988statistical}, meaning there is a 60--70\\% probability of failing to detect a true effect of this magnitude at $\\alpha{=}0.05$. We report this limitation transparently.\n\nHowever, the study's evidential strength does not rest on any single pairwise comparison. The consistent gradient flow effect (both BN and LN rows show lower CAR under gradient flow ON than OFF) provides the primary evidence for the dual main effects, complemented by the large observed effect size (Cohen's $d{=}-0.93$) in the fully powered BN row. Seed expansion ($n{=}5$ per factorial cell) is underway to increase per-cell power.\n\n\\textbf{Formal $2{\\times}2$ ANOVA.} Table~\\ref{tab:anova} reports the Type~II factorial analysis ($n{=}3$--$4$ per cell, 14 total observations). The interaction is negligible ($F(1,10){=}0.10$, $p{=}0.763$, $\\eta^2{=}0.005$), confirming additive main effects. The normalization main effect approaches significance ($F(1,10){=}4.49$, $p{=}0.060$, $\\eta^2{=}0.253$); the gradient flow main effect is medium-sized but non-significant ($F(1,10){=}3.20$, $p{=}0.104$, $\\eta^2{=}0.180$). The non-significant gradient flow $p$-value reflects limited per-cell power rather than an absent effect.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{$2{\\times}2$ factorial ANOVA (Type~II SS). The interaction is negligible; the normalization main effect approaches significance ($p{=}0.060$).}\n\\label{tab:anova}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nSource & $F$ & df & $p$ & $\\eta^2$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGradient flow (ON vs.\\ OFF) & 3.20 & 1,\\,10 & 0.104 & 0.180 \\\\\nNormalization (BN vs.\\ LN) & 4.49 & 1,\\,10 & 0.060 & 0.253 \\\\\nInteraction & 0.10 & 1,\\,10 & 0.763 & 0.005 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Pairwise effect sizes.} Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Cohen's $d$ for all factorial cell pairs and versus Vanilla ($n{=}3$--$4$ per cell).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Pairwise Cohen's $d$ for factorial cells and Vanilla.}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nComparison & Cohen's $d$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCell~1 (Full OCC BN) vs.\\ Cell~2 (Full OCC+LN) & $-$1.05 \\\\\nCell~1 vs.\\ Cell~3 (Detach OCC) & $-$0.93 \\\\\nCell~1 vs.\\ Cell~4 (Detach+LN) & $-$1.60 \\\\\nCell~2 vs.\\ Cell~3 & $+$0.25 \\\\\nCell~2 vs.\\ Cell~4 & $-$1.12 \\\\\nCell~3 vs.\\ Cell~4 & $-$1.47 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nCell~1 vs.\\ Vanilla & $-$0.42 \\\\\nCell~2 vs.\\ Vanilla & $+$1.07 \\\\\nCell~3 vs.\\ Vanilla & $+$0.88 \\\\\nCell~4 vs.\\ Vanilla & $+$2.36 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Per-Task Factorial Analysis}\n\\label{app:per_task_factorial}\n\nThe three physically constrained tasks (T3: \\texttt{cookie\\_box}, T5: \\texttt{ramekin}, T6: \\texttt{next\\_to\\_cookie\\_box}) amplify both main effects by approximately $3{\\times}$ relative to the overall means.\n\n\\textbf{Normalization effect amplification.} The overall normalization effect ($\\Delta{=}+5.2$--$5.9$\\,pp between BN and LN) rises to $\\Delta{=}+14.9$--$18.1$\\,pp on constrained tasks. The LN ${>}$ BN direction is consistent across all three constrained tasks ($d{=}-0.39$ to $-0.96$).\n\n\\textbf{Gradient flow on constrained tasks.} On T5 (ramekin), the best auxiliary condition versus Vanilla yields $d{=}2.65$, the largest per-task effect size observed. T5 also shows a reversed gradient flow direction ($d{=}+1.25$, ON ${>}$ OFF), but this reflects near-floor performance (CAR $0.04$--$0.19$) where absolute differences are small and floor artifacts are expected.\n\nThese per-task patterns suggest that the safety-relevant design choices (normalization type, gradient flow) are disproportionately consequential on physically constrained tasks where collision avoidance is most difficult.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\nOur study uses a single architecture (OpenVLA-OFT with LoRA rank 32); full fine-tuning may alter the interaction between auxiliary heads and encoder optimization because all parameters update simultaneously, potentially changing the gradient flow dynamics we observe. All experiments are conducted in MuJoCo simulation on a single task suite (SafeLIBERO Level~I, 10 tasks drawn from LIBERO-Spatial); whether the normalization-dependent gradient flow effect generalizes to other task distributions remains open. LIBERO-Object, which involves different spatial constraints, does not provide ground-truth occupancy labels, preventing factorial comparison on that benchmark; generating synthetic labels is straightforward but was not pursued in the current study. Real-world deployment introduces additional sources of distributional shift that could interact differently with auxiliary head training dynamics. The benchmark contains only static obstacles; dynamic obstacle scenarios may induce time-varying distributions where auxiliary head dynamics behave differently. Our collision metric is binary (L1 displacement threshold $10^{-3}$), which does not capture collision severity or near-miss behavior; a continuous safety metric might reveal finer-grained differences between conditions. CKA measures linear representational similarity, which may miss nonlinear structure relevant to safety behavior. The factorial design uses $n{=}3$--$4$ seeds per cell, which limits statistical power for individual pairwise tests (post-hoc power $\\approx$ 0.3--0.4 for the observed BN-row effect size); we report effect sizes as the primary inferential quantity rather than relying on $p$-values. Larger-scale replication would further strengthen conclusions. Cutoff conditions have 1--3 seeds, limiting temporal conclusions to preliminary observations. All auxiliary conditions use a fixed loss weight $\\lambda_0{=}0.01$ with exponential decay; we did not sweep this hyperparameter, so the gradient flow effect may vary under different loss weight schedules.\n",
